# output:
You're a student learning one subject deeply enough to both understand it and draw it beautifully in painterly pixel art: **snowy mountains at distance: snowfields, couloirs, cornices and glacier over rock, seen from miles away**.

voxsim has never had a convincing snowy mountain. The five real places see them constantly:

- Mount Stuart and Dragontail over the Enchantments;
- Rainier and Baker on clear horizons;
- the Olympics behind the coast;
- Glacier Peak from the Skykomish.

The work: how snow really lies on steep rock (it sheds from steep faces and collects in gullies, on ledges and in bowls), seasonal snowline and glaciers, cornices and wind scour, and how a snow peak reads in value and colour from 5, 20 and 80 km through haze. Then draw it, matching real photos of named peaks.

**Two halves, done together.**

- **Observe how it works.** Study the real thing from real sources: photos, video stills, field guides, scientific and historical literature. Find out what it is, why it looks the way it does, how it changes with season, weather and time, and how it behaves.
- **Translate it into pixels.** Find a method that draws it at every scale voxsim shows: close scene (~0.1 m/px), landscape, 30° iso and region (~1 m/px). Draw from what you observed.

The observation should be good enough that a simulation could use it, and the drawing good enough to sit beside the photos.

**The ambition, stated plainly:** match the reference: first its form (the real silhouettes, where the big lights and darks fall, what sits where), then its finer detail. Don't settle for "approximate it with reasonable effort". This is a safe space to fail many times on the way.

**Ways in that have worked here**, yours to use:

- **Break it down and solve bottom-up.** One crop, one form, one mass at a time, each right against the photo before combining.
- **Measure deeply.** Downsample the photo to your target resolution and treat it as an answer key. Extract its notan (2–3 value groups), value histogram, edge and orientation maps. Diff your work against it. Build any small tools that help you see. Numbers are instruments, not judges: the willow student's best-scoring version was its worst foliage.
- **Call critics.** You have the Agent tool: spawn a fresh critic sub-agent whenever you want cold eyes on a photo | yours pair. Give it just the images and ask what's wrong, most important first. Do it often.

**Photos are the standard.**

- Close-read real photos at every distance: ground-level, oblique aerial (Bing Maps bird's-eye, Google Earth 3D, WA Ecology's shoreline obliques; view for study, don't bulk-scrape) and top-down.
- `python3 ~/work/pixelart-studies/tools/imgfetch.py "<query>" -n 12 -o <dir> --sheet` fetches openly licensed photos (use `--src openverse` if Wikimedia returns 429s).
- Mark Ferrari's pixel art is a fine reference for colour, value contrast and micro-form technique. The photos, not Ferrari, decide what's true.

**Read the art school first, and take real

# Ready to use: techniques from the studio (2026-09-25 night)

Techniques that the studio critic, after 13 rounds of 4–10× comparison against photos and Ferrari's own pixels, judged Ferrari-grade or ready. Each links to the student's work, which has the code and the lessons. Offered to the voxsim painting lanes as a resource, not a spec.

**Palette as light** (all students). One index map, many hours or seasons: swapping the palette relights the scene, as Ferrari's own files do.

- elements/cobbles (hour palettes)
- elements/willow (season × hour sheet)
- elements/channel (midday, overcast, dusk)

**Water** (elements/channel; LESSONS.md, src/technique.py; best image: out/s5_low_dusk_4x.png)

- Water is the sky's own ramp, indexed by reflected elevation, with checker as the water's material.
- Near edge light, far edge dark, with stepped bites.
- Reflections copy the painted pixels, then compress the values.
- A sheen band under the far bank.
- Far channels are one-row runs; detail comes from the row footprint.
- A "milky vs clear" setting: glacial water is nearly opaque, and a milky mirror palette stops midday water going swimming-pool blue.

**Stones and gravel** (elements/cobbles; cobbles_api.py; best: img/sheet_A_hour_x_distance.png)

- Darkness goes only in the pockets between stones, never as an outline.
- Stones are lopsided, faceted 3-D shapes, not ellipses.
- A stone → tick → grain distance ladder with no pop.
- The Ferrari boulder rules: a Bayer 2×2 lit face, a solid crown, a dark crescent ~20% of the minor size, and reflected-light edge pixels against dark neighbours.

**Cast shadows** (elements/willow, elements/driftwood). The ground itself, two steps darker, with its texture kept inside.

**Contact** (elements/willow). Grass overlapping the foot of a bush or stone.

**Willow leaves** (elements/willow). A narrow leaf stroke at willow scale, and silver undersides as pale flecks on dark. David thinks the willow may exceed the existing trees.

**Wood** (elements/driftwood; src/driftwood.py)

- A five-class cylinder shading order proven against Ferrari's own V09 trunk, with the lit band kept free of checker.
- Backlit rims.
- Grain, checks and knots drawn as clean runs.
- Piles separated by value.
- Wet wood.

**Grass zoom** (elements/grass-lod). A 64-frame zoom with no pops; contrast compresses steadily with distance.

**Far and iso** (aerial/; lib/tech_*.py; measure/). Measured canopy rules (crown spacing 2.2–2.9 lit-cap radii, lit share 0.35–0.56, conifers

# How to paint willow scrub

*A tutorial by the willow student. The story of how I got here, dead ends included, is in [POST.md](POST.md); my raw working thread is in [the notebook](../../notebooks/willow/notebook.md).*

## What I chose to teach, and why

I could have written one long walkthrough of my painter. I don't think that's what a reader needs from me, because the painter is a stack of about ten separate techniques, and most of them are useful on their own: the clump grammar works for any leafy mass, the contact rules work for anything that stands on gravel, the palette system works for anything at all. So this is a **front page and ten short how-tos**, each one technique, taught from the study's real code (snapshotted verbatim in [`code/`](code/)) and the real images it produced, including the failure it replaced. They're ordered the way the painter runs, but each stands alone.

I left out things that didn't earn their place: the physics bush I started with, the random parameter search, and the gravel (other students do gravel better).

![One willow, layer by layer](img/t_construction.png)

*The whole painter in one picture: `willow.paint_willow` with later layers switched off, same seed throughout. [`code/figs.py`](code/figs.py) makes this and every `t_` figure from the real functions.*

## The idea in one paragraph

Foliage is a heap of **clumps**. Each clump is lit on the side toward the sun and dark along its bottom, where it tucks under the clump in front. Paint the clumps as a *light field*, not as colour, then draw small marks from that field: dense and bright on clump caps, sparse and dim below, none in the dark bands. The mass stays calm in value and all the contrast lives inside the marks. The edge of the mass is where the clumps end, lit on top. That's Ferrari's hedge. A willow is that grammar laid on a willow's own anatomy (a fan of arching wands from a root crown), with a willow's own mark (narrow leaves hanging off a twig), sitting in the world (a contact line, a pocket, the ground's own shadow).

# How-to 4: The far view: haze, bands and palette economy

*[Back to the field guide](../TUTORIAL.md). Code: `~/work/pixelart-studies/aerial/lib/painter.py` (bands, cavity tone, band collapse, sky), `lib/pal.py` (lights, haze, budget), `lib/subjects.py: range_valley`. Plates: `plates/range_far*.png`. Sheet: `sheets/sheet_far.png`.*

This is how the evening range was made: a perspective camera 3.3 km up, pitched 24° down over a 9 km range with a glacial valley and a river. The plate below was the one singled out.

![Evening range](../img/plate_range_far_evening.png)

## 1. What the photos say

Measured row by row down a high oblique (Blue Ridge, [how-to 1](01-measure.md#step-5-haze-row-by-row)), from near to far:

- the **black point rises** from L\* 0.5 to 48;
- the **value range collapses** from 45 to 9;
- b\* goes from 0 to −14, turning blue.

Far land is two close, lifted, blue values, a lit and a shade. That's also how Ferrari's far masses behave: the master-copy student found that his lit colour holds with distance and his shadow colour shifts.

## 2. Depth bands, with dithered seams

Split the ray distance into bands (here 2.5, 4.5 and 7 km), and give each band a haze mix toward the light's haze colour (0 / 22 / 45 / 68%). Mixing toward a light haze lifts the black point first and compresses the range, as measured.



band = band_of(G[&quot;t&quot;] * (1 + (thr_4 - 0.5) * 0.06), bands)     # jitter distance by a Bayer threshold: dithered seams
...
def haze_mix(rgb, light, f):
    return rgb * (1 - f) + light.haze * f




The band edges have to be dithered, or each one is a hard milky ring across the valley (notebook 083). The jitter amount matters. At 14% the seams were wide checker bands that looked like smoke (084), and at 6% they're a one- or two-pixel dither.

![Bands](../img/fig_far_bands.png)

*`code/figs.py far`, evening. Left: no depth bands, a dark heavy picture in 32 colours. Middle: the band map, where the lightest grey is the farthest band (the sky is band 0 and shows darkest). Right: with bands, band collapse and the budget, 64 colours. The near valley keeps its full range, and the far peaks go to two lifted violet values.*

## 3. Band collapse

Aerial perspective removes steps, not just contrast. So in band 2 each material keeps only its two family tones plus its darkest accent, and in band 3 only lit and shade:



for b_ in (2, 3):
    mb = (cv.band == b_) &amp; (cv.mat &gt; 0) &amp; (cv.mat != MAT_ID[&quot;sky&quot;])
    for m_ in np.unique(cv.mat[mb]):
        n_ = MATERIALS[[k for k, v in MAT_ID.items() if v == m_][0]][&quot;steps&quot;]; ns_ = n_ // 2
        sel = mb &amp; (cv.mat == m_); st_ = cv.step[sel]
        lo_keep = 0 if b_ == 2 else ns_ - 1
        cv.step[sel] = np.where(st_ &gt;= ns_, np.minimum(st_, ns_ + 1), np.maximum(np.minimum(st_, ns_ - 1), lo_keep))




This happens on the index canvas, so it's independent of the hour.

## 4. Cavity tone draws the mountain

At a few km, n·l alone gives mountains as soft blobs. What draws their str

# output:
# How to pixel-art snow, by construction

*Snowfields, gullies and ledges, capped boulders, snowy forest, falling snow: a method you can write as a program.*

Snow is the easiest thing in a landscape to get wrong in pixels, because it seems to have no colour and no texture of its own. The obvious move is to treat it as a material: paint the terrain, then shade it white. That gives you a mesh wearing a white texture. It has no form, and it ignores where snow actually goes.

In Mark Ferrari's winter scenes and Kazuo Oga's backgrounds, snow is a set of **objects**. It sits in loaves on boulders, in slabs on boughs, in tongues down gullies and in sheets across open ground. Each has a silhouette and a few flat values, and each is placed by gravity and wind. This post turns that observation into a grammar, and proves it with a program (`snowpaint.py`, `scenes.py`, `sheets.py`) that paints every image below from a scene spec.

Everything here is 640×360 or smaller, with 64 colours or fewer per frame (enforced), shown upscaled with nearest neighbour.

---

## 1. What the references teach

I studied the three Ferrari pieces supplied and five more of his colour-cycling scenes. I rendered those from the original palette-and-pixel data, so these crops are at **native resolution**: Winter Forest, Ice Wind, Mountain Storm, Mountain Fortress. I also looked at Kazuo Oga's approach (as described in interviews: a small palette, extract the essence of the scene and push it) and at photographs of snow on boulders, streams and couloirs.

### 1.1 Caps are loaves wider than their rock

![Ferrari boulders, native x4](refs/zoom_winter_boulders_native.png)

- The cap's **top outline is a flat-topped, rounded loaf**. It ignores the rock's own bumps and peaks, because snow bridges dips and rounds off at its edges.
- **The cap is wider than the rock** at its lower rim. It bulges past the silhouette and curls under.
- Three bands, top to bottom: a **light top plane** (it faces the sky), a **cooler front roll**, and a **one-pixel dark under-rim**. Right under the rim, a **hard dark shadow** falls on the rock.
- **Where the snow stops on the rock is a level line, not an offset from the rock's outline.** Seen from a low eye it is nearly horizontal, with a slight forward sag. This one observation fixed my caps (see §5).
- Under forest cover, caps are **mid lavender, not white**. The brightest snow in the picture is the open path and the far opening.
- Boulders come in **overlapping clusters**, painted back to front. Each rock keeps its own cap, and a front cap's lit top edge is set against the dark under-rim of the cap behind.

### 1.2 Boughs carry slabs, and the tips shed

![Ferrari branches](refs/zoom_branches_left.png)

A snow slab rides the top of each bough and stops short of the tip, where the bough droops too steeply to hold it. The lower edge drips in fingers, and dark needles show underneath. **Bough snow is the same cap rule on a thin support.**

# Washington rock, from photos to pixels

A student notebook. The subject: the rock of the five voxsim places (Enchantments granite,
Skykomish granite and gneiss, San Juan greenstone/chert/sandstone shores, Olympic coast mudstone and
sandstone stacks, Yakima Canyon basalt). The two halves: how the rock works (why it breaks, weathers,
carries life and reads at each distance), and a painter that draws it at voxsim's four scales.

**Where this stands, plainly.** The observation half is solid and has numbers a simulation can use.
The drawing half is a working *method* (joints in metres → geometry → a two-family index canvas →
palettes per hour), with photo | mine pairs and full sweeps. Beside the photos, the granite wall and
talus and the CRB colonnade read as the real thing at 1×; the sea stack and the San Juan shore read
as rock but not yet as *those* rocks; region scale and the talus matrix are the weakest cells. None
of it is at Ferrari's 8× bar. Details per stage, and the open list at the end.



cd src
nice -n 10 python3 iterate.py granite_wall r1        # photo | mine, 3 hours  -&gt; stages/iter/
nice -n 10 python3 sheets.py A B C D E               # the sweeps              -&gt; sheets/
nice -n 10 python3 measure.py                        # photo colour/value boxes -&gt; img/measure_crops.png




Everything runs on one core in seconds per cell (numba rasterizer + numpy); peak RSS ~0.9 GB on the
biggest region cells. No Modal needed.

Sources are listed at the end of each subsection; the naturalist studio's geology guides
(`~/work/naturalist/geo/02, 03, 05, 08, 09`) did most of the literature work and I lean on them,
checked against photos I looked at myself (`refs/`, `~/work/naturalist/geo/photos/`) and a few
papers I read for the numbers they didn't carry.

### 1.1 The one idea: joints decide everything you see

Every rock face in these five places is made of pieces of **joint planes**. A joint is a fracture

# output:
# The alpine and the meadows: frost, snow, and the ground above and between the trees

*Geo field guide 10. For the sim lanes and the painters. Written 2026-09-25 by the studio geologist. The plants desk covers the vegetation of these places; this is the ground under it.*

---

## 1. The alpine zone: what the ground does above treeline

Treeline in Washington is roughly **1,500 to 1,700 m in the Olympics, ~1,800 m on the west slope of the Cascades, and 2,100 to 2,400 m on the drier east side** (plants desk for detail). Above it, the ground is shaped by **frost, snow and gravity**, with glaciers on the highest peaks (guide 03).

### Processes

| process | what it does | rate / size | where |
|---|---|---|---|
| **frost shattering** | freeze-thaw wedging breaks bare rock into blocks and grit | cliff retreat 0.1-1 mm/yr (guide 05) | every bare rock face; peaks in spring and autumn |
| **frost creep and gelifluction (solifluction)** | soil heaves up perpendicular to the slope when it freezes, settles vertically when it thaws, and flows when saturated by snowmelt over still-frozen ground | surface movement **0.5 to 5 cm/yr** (Matsuoka 2001, global review) | wet alpine slopes 5-25 deg: **solifluction lobes and terraces** with steep turf-covered fronts 0.3-1.5 m high |
| **needle ice** | ice needles grow at the surface on clear frosty nights, lifting grains and seedlings | cm per night | bare soil in meadows and on trails, autumn and spring |
| **sorting (patterned ground)** | repeated freezing sorts stones from fines | stone circles 1-3 m across on flats, **stone stripes** on slopes | alpine flats and saddles, best east of the crest; mostly relict from colder times |
| **nivation** | snowpatches that linger into summer enhance frost weathering and meltwater wash around their edges | hollows 10-100 m | lee slopes below ridges (north and east aspects) |
| **snow avalanches** | scour paths through forest, carry rock and trees, build debris cones | paths 10s of m wide, km long | every steep slope over ~30 deg with snow; hydro owns the snowpack |
| **rock glaciers** | tongues or lobes of talus with ice inside, creeping downslope like slow lava | **~159 mapped in the eastern Cascades**, mostly tongue-shaped, talus-derived, at cirque headwalls in the colder north-east (Weidenaar/CWU; Geomorphology 2021) | bases of steep cirque walls east of the crest |
| **protalus ramparts** | ridges of boulders at the foot of snowpatches (guide 05) | a few m high | below talus |

### Landforms at a glance

- **Fellfield**: flat or gently sloping stony ground, 50-90% stones, cushion plants between; ridge crests and saddles.
- **Rock glacier**: a lobe of bouldery rubble 100 m to 1 km long with a **steep, fresh-looking front** (35-45 deg) of loose blocks, ridges and furrows on its surface curving like flow lines. Active ones have raw fronts with fines spilling out; relict ones are vegetated and slumped.
- **Solifluction lobes**: tongues and terraces of turf-covered soil 1-20 m

"""dem.py -- real terrain for the match-the-photo work: the Enchantments lidar and 10 m DEM that
lane-place-enchant already prepared (read-only), resampled to one UTM 10N grid, plus a fast
numba heightfield ray-caster for camera search (sky mask + depth).

    Z, x0, y0, res = load_enchant(res=2.0)          # metres, row 0 = north
    sky, depth = cast(Z, x0, y0, res, cam_e, cam_n, cam_h, az, pitch, hfov, W, H)
"""
import numpy as np, os
from numba import njit
from scipy import ndimage as ndi

DEMDIR = os.path.expanduser("~/work/voxsim-wt/place-enchant/studies/enchant/dem/")
CACHE = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)), "../cache")


def load_enchant(res=2.0, box=(661500, 666300, 5258800, 5263200)):
    """merged DEM on a UTM10N grid: 1 m lidar where it exists (upper core), 10 m DEM elsewhere."""
    os.makedirs(CACHE, exist_ok=True)
    fn = os.path.join(CACHE, f"enchant_{res:g}_{'_'.join(map(str, box))}.npy")
    e0, e1, n0, n1 = box
    if os.path.exists(fn):
        return np.load(fn), e0, n1, res
    from pyproj import Transformer
    xs = np.arange(e0, e1, res) + res / 2
    ys = np.arange(n1, n0, -res) - res / 2            # row 0 = north
    X, Y = np.meshgrid(xs, ys)
    t = Transformer.from_crs("EPSG:32610", "EPSG:4326", always_xy=True)
    lon, lat = t.transform(X, Y)
    c10 = np.load(DEMDIR + "core10.npy").astype(np.float64)
    # core10 transform: lon = -120.87 + col*dx ; lat = 47.52 - row*dy (pixel corners)
    dx, dy = 9.259259269220167e-05, 9.25925927753796e-05
    col = (lon + 120.87) / dx - 0.5; row = (47.52 - lat) / dy - 0.5
    Z = ndi.map_coordinates(c10, [row, col], order=1, mode="nearest")
    # upper 1 m lidar: 663100..666300 E, 5259300..5261350 N, row0 = north
    up = np.load(DEMDIR + "upper1m.npy", mmap_mode="r")
    ue0, ue1, un0, un1 = 663100, 666300, 5259300, 5261350
    m = (X > ue0 + 2) & (X < ue1 - 2) & (Y > un0 + 2) & (Y < un1 - 2)
    if m.any():
        ur = (un1 - Y[m]) - 0.5; uc = (X[m] - ue0) - 0.5
        upz = ndi.map_coordinates(np.asarray(up, np.float64), [ur, uc], order=1)
        ok = np.isfinite(upz) & (upz > 500)
        zz = Z[m]; zz[ok] = upz[ok]
        # feather the seam over 60 m
        dist = np.minimum.reduce([X[m] - ue0, ue1 - X[m], Y[m] - un0, un1 - Y[m]])
        w = np.clip(dist / 60.0, 0, 1)
        Z[m] = w * zz + (1 - w) * Z[m]
    Z = Z.astype(np.float32)
    np.save(fn, Z)
    return Z, e0, n1, res


def cam_basis(az, pitch):
    a, p = np.radians(az), np.radians(pitch)
    fwd = np.array([np.sin(a) * np.cos(p), np.cos(a) * np.cos(p), np.sin(p)])
    right = np.array([np.cos(a), -np.sin(a), 0.0])
    up = np.cross(right, fwd)
    return fwd, right, up


@njit(cache=True)
def _cast(Z, x0, y0, res, ce, cn, cz, D, far, step0):
    H, W = D.shape[0], D.shape[1]
    ny, nx = Z.shape
    depth = np.full((H, W), np.inf)
    for i in range(H):
        for j in range(W):
            dx, dy, dz = D[i, j, 0], D[i, j, 1], D[i, j, 2]

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""imgfetch: fast, polite reference-image sourcing (Wikimedia Commons + Openverse).

  python3 imgfetch.py "cinder cone scoria slope" -n 12 -o refs/cinder
  python3 imgfetch.py -f queries.txt -n 8 -o refs            # one query per line; subdir per query
  options: --src commons,openverse   --width 1200   --sheet (writes <out>/_sheet.jpg)

Writes images plus <out>/sources.tsv (file, title, license, author, page url).
Cached: files already present are skipped. Handles 429/5xx with Retry-After backoff.
Parallel downloads (4 workers) with a shared per-host rate limiter (~3 req/s; Wikimedia's media
host is kept to 1 per 2 s and slows further on any 429, since it throttles hard per IP after bursts).
Openverse (mostly Flickr-hosted CC images) is the primary source because it stays fast.
"""
import argparse, concurrent.futures as cf, json, os, re, sys, threading, time, urllib.error, urllib.parse, urllib.request

UA = "pixelart-studies-imgfetch/1.0 (research; contact: local)"
_locks = {}; _last = {}; _glock = threading.Lock()

SLOW_HOSTS = {"upload.wikimedia.org": 2.0}   # Wikimedia media host: ~1 req/s or it 429s

def _throttle(host, min_gap=0.34):
    min_gap = SLOW_HOSTS.get(host, min_gap)
    with _glock:
        lk = _locks.setdefault(host, threading.Lock())
    with lk:
        dt = time.time() - _last.get(host, 0)
        if dt < min_gap:
            time.sleep(min_gap - dt)
        _last[host] = time.time()

def get(url, tries=4, timeout=25):
    host = urllib.parse.urlparse(url).netloc
    if host == "upload.wikimedia.org":
        tries = 2  # fail fast; other sources fill in
    for i in range(tries):
        _throttle(host)
        try:
            req = urllib.request.Request(url, headers={"User-Agent": UA})
            return urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=timeout).read()
        except urllib.error.HTTPError as e:
            if e.code == 429:
                with _glock:
                    SLOW_HOSTS[host] = min(8.0, SLOW_HOSTS.get(host, 0.34) * 2)  # adapt: this host wants us slower
            if e.code in (429, 500, 502, 503, 504) and i < tries - 1:
                wait = min(15, int(e.headers.get("Retry-After") or 0) or 2 * 2 ** i)
                time.sleep(wait); continue
            raise
        except (urllib.error.URLError, TimeoutError):
            if i < tries - 1:
                time.sleep(2 * (i + 1)); continue
            raise

def commons(query, n, width):
    q = urllib.parse.urlencode({"action": "query", "generator": "search", "gsrnamespace": 6, "gsrlimit": min(50, n * 3),
                                "gsrsearch": query + " filetype:bitmap", "prop": "imageinfo",
                                "iiprop": "url|size|extmetadata", "iiurlwidth": min((b for b in (330, 500, 960, 1280) if b >= width), default=1280), "format": "json"})
    d = json.loads(get("https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/api.php?" + q))
    out = []
    for p in sorted(d.get("query", {}).get("pages", {}).values(), key=lambda

# output:
You're a student learning one subject deeply enough to both understand it and draw it beautifully in painterly pixel art: **snowy mountains at distance: snowfields, couloirs, cornices and glacier over rock, seen from miles away**.

voxsim has never had a convincing snowy mountain. The five real places see them constantly:

- Mount Stuart and Dragontail over the Enchantments;
- Rainier and Baker on clear horizons;
- the Olympics behind the coast;
- Glacier Peak from the Skykomish.

The work: how snow really lies on steep rock (it sheds from steep faces and collects in gullies, on ledges and in bowls), seasonal snowline and glaciers, cornices and wind scour, and how a snow peak reads in value and colour from 5, 20 and 80 km through haze. Then draw it, matching real photos of named peaks.

**Two halves, done together.**

- **Observe how it works.** Study the real thing from real sources: photos, video stills, field guides, scientific and historical literature. Find out what it is, why it looks the way it does, how it changes with season, weather and time, and how it behaves.
- **Translate it into pixels.** Find a method that draws it at every scale voxsim shows: close scene (~0.1 m/px), landscape, 30° iso and region (~1 m/px). Draw from what you observed.

The observation should be good enough that a simulation could use it, and the drawing good enough to sit beside the photos.

**The ambition, stated plainly:** match the reference: first its form (the real silhouettes, where the big lights and darks fall, what sits where), then its finer detail. Don't settle for "approximate it with reasonable effort". This is a safe space to fail many times on the way.

**Ways in that have worked here**, yours to use:

- **Break it down and solve bottom-up.** One crop, one form, one mass at a time, each right against the photo before combining.
- **Measure deeply.** Downsample the photo to your target resolution and treat it as an answer key. Extract its notan (2–3 value groups), value histogram, edge and orientation maps. Diff your work against it. Build any small tools that help you see. Numbers are instruments, not judges: the willow student's best-scoring version was its worst foliage.
- **Call critics.** You have the Agent tool: spawn a fresh critic sub-agent whenever you want cold eyes on a photo | yours pair. Give it just the images and ask what's wrong, most important first. Do it often.

**Photos are the standard.**

- Close-read real photos at every distance: ground-level, oblique aerial (Bing Maps bird's-eye, Google Earth 3D, WA Ecology's shoreline obliques; view for study, don't bulk-scrape) and top-down.
- `python3 ~/work/pixelart-studies/tools/imgfetch.py "<query>" -n 12 -o <dir> --sheet` fetches openly licensed photos (use `--src openverse` if Wikimedia returns 429s).
- Mark Ferrari's pixel art is a fine reference for colour, value contrast and micro-form technique. The photos, not Ferrari, decide what's true.

# voxsim infrastructure: where work runs, where things live

For every lane. Read this once. Everything here is meant to be **easy and fast**. If any step takes more than about 10 minutes of fiddling, stop and say so in your NOTES and your report, and ask for help. Don't grind on infrastructure.

## Credentials

- `~/work/.env.local` holds Modal and AWS keys. Load them with `set -a; . ~/work/.env.local; set +a`.
- **Never print, echo, log or commit their values.**

## Where compute runs
| Work | Where |
|---|---|
| Reading, writing code, small checks, `cargo check` on one crate | Your worktree, locally. `.cargo/config.toml` caps builds at 2 jobs. |
| Release builds of the workspace, long sims or soaks, render sweeps, heavy numpy | **Modal** (below). The laptop crashed from memory pressure with 14 lanes building at once, so don't run more than one heavy local job at a time. |
| Self-contained work that only needs the repo | Cloud agents; the manager decides. |

## Modal

- **Workspace:** set `MODAL_ENVIRONMENT=main` after loading the env file (the local `~/.modal.toml` defaults to another workspace's environment). A probe container with 16 CPUs came up in about 6 s.
- **Image:** use `modal.Image.from_registry("rust:1-bookworm", add_python="3.12")`. It ships current Rust. `debian_slim().apt_install(...)` failed on a stale Debian security mirror, so don't use it for Rust.
- **Budget:** the whole project has about $30 of Modal credit for now.
  - Size containers to the job, and set `timeout=`.
  - Shut down what you start (no idle `keep_warm`).
  - Log each Modal run's resources and wall time in your NOTES, so spend can be tracked.
  - If a job will take more than ~1 CPU-hour, say so before you run it.
- **Volumes:** use them for joint disk-and-CPU work (a cargo target cache per lane, shared data). Name everything `voxsim-<lane>-<what>` (e.g. `voxsim-place-yakima-target`), so nothing sprawls untracked.
- **Tracking:** record every app, volume and S3 prefix you create in your lane's NOTES under "Remote resou
Write: elements/snowpeak/src/dem.py
"""dem.py -- real terrain for the snowpeak study.

USGS 3DEP 1/3" (~10 m) tiles and ESA WorldCover 2021 (class 70 = snow and ice) from
~/work/voxsim-data (read-only), reprojected onto one UTM 10N grid at a chosen resolution.

    T = load("rainier", half_km=12, res=20)      # dict: Z (m), ice (0/1), x0 (west edge E), y0 (north edge N), res
    T = load_box(e0, e1, n0, n1, res)

Row 0 is north.  Memory: a 60x60 km box at 30 m is 2000^2 float32 = 16 MB; the source tiles are
read through windowed reads by rasterio's reproject, so peak RSS stays well under 2 GB.
"""
import os, glob
import numpy as np
import rasterio
from rasterio.warp import reproject, Resampling
from rasterio.transform import from_origin
from pyproj import Transformer

DATA = os.path.expanduser("~/work/voxsim-data")
CACHE = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)), "../cache")
UTM = "EPSG:32610"
_to_utm = Transformer.from_crs("EPSG:4326", UTM, always_xy=True)
_to_ll = Transformer.from_crs(UTM, "EPSG:4326", always_xy=True)

PEAKS = {  # summit lat, lon, elevation (m) -- USGS/GNIS
    "rainier": (46.8529, -121.7604, 4392),
    "stuart": (47.4751, -120.9024, 2870),
    "dragontail": (47.4787, -120.8322, 2694),
    "olympus": (47.8013, -123.7108, 2432),
    "glacierpeak": (48.1125, -121.1139, 3213),
    "baker": (48.7768, -121.8144, 3286),
}


def ll2utm(lat, lon):
    return _to_utm.transform(lon, lat)


def utm2ll(e, n):
    lon, lat = _to_ll.transform(e, n)
    return lat, lon


def _mosaic(files, dst, transform, resampling, nodata_fill):
    for f in files:
        with rasterio.open(f) as src:
            tmp = np.full(dst.shape, np.nan, np.float32)
            reproject(rasterio.band(src, 1), tmp, src_transform=src.transform, src_crs=src.crs,
                      dst_transform=transform, dst_crs=UTM, resampling=resampling,
                      src_nodata=src.nodata, dst_nodata=np.nan)
            ok = np.isfinite(tmp) & ~np.isfinite(dst)
            dst[ok] = tmp[ok]
    dst[~np.isfinite(dst)] = nodata_fill
    return dst


def load_box(e0, e1, n0, n1, res, tag=None, ice=True):
    os.makedirs(CACHE, exist_ok=True)
    key = tag or f"{int(e0)}_{int(e1)}_{int(n0)}_{int(n1)}"
    fn = os.path.join(CACHE, f"dem_{key}_{res:g}.npz")
    if os.path.exists(fn):
        d = np.load(fn)
        return dict(Z=d["Z"], ice=d["ice"], x0=float(d["x0"]), y0=float(d["y0"]), res=float(d["res"]))
    W = int(round((e1 - e0) / res)); H = int(round((n1 - n0) / res))
    transform = from_origin(e0, n1, res, res)
    Z = np.full((H, W), np.nan, np.float32)
    Z = _mosaic(sorted(glob.glob(DATA + "/dem10/USGS_13_*.tif")), Z, transform,
                Resampling.average if res > 15 else Resampling.bilinear, 0.0)
    I = np.zeros((H, W), np.float32)
    if ice:
        I[:] = np.nan
        I = _mosaic_class(sorted(glob.glob(DATA + "/landcover/ESA_WorldCover_*.tif")), I, transform)
    np.savez_compressed(fn, Z=Z, ice=I, x0=e0, y0=n1, res=res)
    return dict(Z=Z, ice=I, x0=float(e0), y0=float(n1), res=float(res))


def _mosaic_class(files, dst, transform, cls=70):
    """fraction of WorldCover pixels of class `cls` in each cell (average of a 0/1 mask)."""
    for f in files:
        with rasterio.open(f) as src:
            # read only the window we need, as 0/1 float
            left, top = transform.c, transform.f
            right = left + transform.a * dst.shape[1]; bottom = top + transform.e * dst.shape[0]
            corners = [_to_ll.transform(x, y) for x in (left, right) for y in (top, bottom)]
            lons = [c[0] for c in corners]; lats = [c[1] for c in corners]
            b = src.bounds
            if max(lons) < b.left or min(lons) > b.right or max(lats) < b.bottom or min(lats) > b.top:
                continue
            win = rasterio.windows.from_bounds(max(min(lons), b.left) - 0.01, max(min(lats), b.bottom) - 0.01,
                                               min(max(lons), b.right) + 0.01, min(max(lats), b.top) + 0.01,
                                               src.transform).round_offsets().round_lengths()
            a = src.read(1, window=win)
            m = (a == cls).astype(np.float32)
            wt = src.window_transform(win)
            tmp = np.full(dst.shape, np.nan, np.float32)
            reproject(m, tmp, src_transform=wt, src_crs=src.crs, dst_transform=transform, dst_crs=UTM,
                      resampling=Resampling.average, dst_nodata=np.nan)
            ok = np.isfinite(tmp) & ~np.isfinite(dst)
            dst[ok] = tmp[ok]
    dst[~np.isfinite(dst)] = 0.0
    return dst


def load(peak, half_km=10, res=20):
    lat, lon, _ = PEAKS[peak]
    e, n = ll2utm(lat, lon)
    h = half_km * 1000
    return load_box(round(e - h), round(e + h), round(n - h), round(n + h), res,
                    tag=f"{peak}_{half_km}")


def sample(T, e, n):
    """bilinear height at UTM points"""
    Z = T["Z"]; r = (T["y0"] - n) / T["res"] - 0.5; c = (e - T["x0"]) / T["res"] - 0.5
    from scipy.ndimage import map_coordinates
    return map_coordinates(Z, [np.atleast_1d(r), np.atleast_1d(c)], order=1, mode="nearest")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    import sys, time
    for p in sys.argv[1:] or ["rainier"]:
        t = time.time(); T = load(p, 10, 20)
        print(p, T["Z"].shape, float(T["Z"].max()), "ice frac", float(T["ice"].mean()), f"{time.time()-t:.1f}s")

Bash: Render hillshade with ice overlay
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/snowpeak/src; cat > quicklook.py <<'EOF'
import numpy as np, dem
from PIL import Image
tiles=[]
for p in ["rainier","stuart","olympus","glacierpeak"]:
    T=dem.load(p,10,20); Z=T["Z"]; gy,gx=np.gradient(Z,20)
    n=np.dstack([-gx,-gy,np.ones_like(Z)]); n/=np.linalg.norm(n,axis=2,keepdims=True)
    L=np.array([-0.5,0.5,0.7]); L/=np.linalg.norm(L); hs=np.clip(n@L,0,1)
    rgb=np.dstack([hs,hs,hs])*200
    ice=T["ice"][...,None]; rgb=rgb*(1-ice*0.6)+ice*0.6*np.array([120,200,255])
    tiles.append(Image.fromarray(rgb.astype(np.uint8)).resize((500,500)))
out=Image.new("RGB",(2000,500)); [out.paste(t,(i*500,0)) for i,t in enumerate(tiles)]
out.save("../img/quicklook_dem_ice.png")
EOF

I found relevant information about SnowSlide, Bernhardt, Schulz, and the exponential equation for snow holding depth. Here are the key findings:

## SnowSlide Overview
SnowSlide (Bernhardt and Schulz, 2010) is a model using a simple parameterization for gravitational snow transport, where avalanches are simulated when a slope threshold and a snow holding capacity are exceeded.

## Snow Holding Depth and Exponential Equation
The maximum snow-holding depth is deep for flat areas, decreases exponentially with increasing slope angle, and is calculated by an exponential regression function (Bernhardt and Schulz, 2010).

An exponential relation between S and SWEmax is applied as proposed by Bernhardt and Schulz (2010), where SWEmax = SS1·exp(SS2·S), where S is the slope angle.

## Practical Examples
The default formulation of the snow-holding depth proposed by Bernhardt and Schulz (2010) leads to a maximal snow thickness (taken perpendicular to the slope) of 3.08 m, 1.11 m, 0.45 m and 0.15 m for slopes of 30°, 45°, 60° and 75°, respectively.

The model was published in Geophysical Research Letters (2010) and has become widely used for simulating snow redistribution on steep mountain terrain.

I've performed the web search and found relevant research on snow cover in steep alpine rock faces. Here are the key findings:

## Snow Cover on Steep Alpine Slopes

Research examined snow profiles taken in 50–65° slopes at elevations between 2900 and 3600 m asl on north- and south-facing slopes in the Swiss Alps, which directly addresses your angle range of interest.

## Snow Retention Characteristics

Measurements showed no decrease of snow depth at slope angles up to 70°, indicating that snow can be retained even on very steep angles. Considerable amounts of snow can accumulate in steep, rough rock walls, with thermal insulation of the ground starting with snow depths exceeding 0.2 m in rock faces.

## Slope Aspect Effects

Snow pits in frozen rock walls revealed that steep slopes have distinctive snowpack characteristics with marked contrasts in snow characteristics between north- and south-facing aspects. Under intense solar radiation, basal ice layers and multiple hard melt-freeze crusts formed on south-facing slopes, while on shady north-facing slopes, thick basal melt-freeze crusts formed when snow persisted during stable weather periods.

## Snow Distribution Variability

The high spatial and temporal variability of snow deposits in steep slopes results from the interaction of topoclimatic factors such as slope angle, sun exposure, and wind transport, creating a highly variable and local thermal effect.

def snd_max_exponential(slp, a, c, min):
    """Function that compute the maximal height of snow each pixel can store based on the slope.
    The function is an exponential and parameters are estimated from 'Bernhardt & Schulz 2007'.

    Parameters
    ----------
    slope: np matrix
        Matrix that attributes a slope value to each pixel
    a: float
        Parameter of the exponential function. Default is -0.14.
    c: float
        Parameter of the exponential function. Default is 145
    min: float
        Minimum snow height each pixel can store regardless of the slope. Default is 0.05.

    Returns
    -------
    A matrix associating to each pixel the maximum heiht of snow it can store without routing (np matrix in meters)
    """

    snd_max = c * np.exp(-a * slp)
    snd_max[snd_max < min] = min

    return snd_max


def snow_routing(snd, snd_max, flow_dir, routing):
    """That function routes the snow based on the routing method chosen ('mfd' or 'd8').
    It is called at each iteration.

    Parameters
    ----------
    snd: np matrix
        Matrix of the snow heights associated to each pixel at iteration k
    snd_max: np matrix
        matrix associating to each pixel the maximum heiht of snow it can store based on an exponential function of slope
    flow_dir: np matrix or list of np matrices (depending on the routing method)
        Matrix of the direction and coefficient of snow that is routed to the next pixels
    routing: str
        Routing method chosen by the users ('mfd' or 'd8')
    """

    # Compute the quantity and fraction of snow that should be routed
    snr = snd - snd_max  # snr is the quantity of snow that can be routed for each pixel
    snr[
        snr < 0
    ] = 0  # Negative values are computed when no amount of snow can be routed
    # Then every negative value is set to 0, if threshold is not reached, the snow isn't routed.

    if routing == "mfd":
        direction_indices = {
            "North": 0,
            "Northeast": 1,
            "East": 2,
            "Southeast": 3,
            "South": 4,
            "Southwest": 5,
            "West": 6,
            "Northwest": 7,
        }
        # Neighbour to the North
        snd[1:-1, 1:-1] += (
            flow_dir[0][2:, 1:-1] * snr[2:, 1:-1]
            - flow_dir[0][1:-1, 1:-1] * snr[1:-1, 1:-1]
        )
        # Neighbour to the NorthEast
        snd[1:-1, 1:-1] += (
            flow_dir[1][2:, :-2] * snr[2:, :-2]
            - flow_dir[1][1:-1, 1:-1] * snr[1:-1, 1:-1]
        )
        # Neighbour to the East
        snd[1:-1, 1:-1] += (
            flow_dir[2][1:-1, :-2] * snr[1:-1, :-2]
            - flow_dir[2][1:-1, 1:-1] * snr[1:-1, 1:-1]
        )
        # Neighbour to the SouthEast
        snd[1:-1, 1:-1] += (
            flow_dir[3][:-2, :-2] * snr[:-2, :-2]
            - flow_dir[3][1:-1, 1:-1] * snr[1:-1, 1:-1]
        )
13:    epsilon=1e-3,
32:    epsilon: float
33:        Condition to get out the loop (convergence is con

I found relevant information about Blue Glacier's equilibrium line altitude and mass balance. Here are the key findings:

## Equilibrium Line Altitude (ELA) Data

Blue Glacier's equilibrium line altitude (ELA) has varied considerably over time, ranging from around 1505 meters in 1974 to 2030 meters in 1981. For example, the present firn limit on Blue Glacier is centered on an altitude of 1,725 m.

## Mass Balance Information

The mass of Blue Glacier has changed less than 7 m (water equivalent) during the 56-year period from 1939 to 1995, which is minor compared with other glaciers in the region and elsewhere in the world.

Glacier-average annual mass balances, beginning in 1956, have been calculated either from stake measurements and probing of late-season snow, or from a regression analysis using late-season measurements of the equilibrium line altitude.

## Ablation Gradient

The net budget gradient of the Blue Glacier ablation zone (lower glacier) is 10 mm·m⁻¹, a figure derived from 1959 and 1960 when the glacier was closest to mass equilibrium.

The search results contain detailed time series data and historical records of Blue Glacier's mass balance measurements spanning from 1956 onwards.

Based on the search results, here's what I found regarding cornice formation, wind speed, overhang growth, and the research by Vogel and Eckerstorfer:

## Wind Speed Conditions for Cornice Formation

Previous field observations have consistently shown that the optimal wind speed range for cornice formation lies between one and two times the threshold wind speed (Eckerstorfer et al., 2013b; Vogel et al., 2012). More specifically, cornice growth is maximized when the wind speed exceeds the threshold value by 40% (Yu et al., 2022).

## Cornice Characteristics and Formation

Snow cornices are leeward-growing masses of snow overhanging and extending horizontally beyond the edge, usually appearing on the ridgeline of steep mountains. Cornices range in size from small wind lips of soft snow to large overhangs of hard snow that are 30 feet (10 meters) or taller.

## Cornice Dimensions from Studies

A wedge-shaped cornice horizontally hanging at the edge had a length of 0.145 m, which is the maximum cornice length observed in previous experiments.

## Growth Stages

The growth of a snow cornice can be divided into several stages, with the initial stage involving a thin slab formation at the mountain edge mainly by adhesion of wind-transported snow particles.

Bash: Fetch Commons metadata for camera GPS
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/snowpeak/refs; cat > commons_meta.py <<'EOF'
import json, urllib.request, urllib.parse, sys, re, glob, time
UA="pixelart-studies/1.0 (research)"
titles=[]
for f in glob.glob("*/sources.tsv"):
    for line in open(f):
        m=re.search(r"commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/(File:[^\t\n]+)",line)
        if m: titles.append(urllib.parse.unquote(m.group(1)).replace("_"," "))
titles=sorted(set(titles)); out={}
for i in range(0,len(titles),20):
    q=urllib.parse.urlencode({"action":"query","titles":"|".join(titles[i:i+20]),"prop":"imageinfo|coordinates","iiprop":"extmetadata|size","coprop":"type|name","colimit":"max","format":"json"})
    d=json.loads(urllib.request.urlopen(urllib.request.Request("https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/api.php?"+q,headers={"User-Agent":UA})).read())
    for p in d["query"]["pages"].values():
        em=p.get("imageinfo",[{}])[0].get("extmetadata",{}); ii=p.get("imageinfo",[{}])[0]
        g=lambda k: re.sub("<[^>]+>","",str(em.get(k,{}).get("value","")))[:200]
        out[p["title"]]=dict(coords=p.get("coordinates"),gps=(g("GPSLatitude"),g("GPSLongitude")),date=g("DateTimeOriginal") or g("DateTime"),desc=g("ImageDescription"),size=(ii.get("width"),ii.get("height")))
    time.sleep(1)
json.dump(out,open("commons_meta.json","w"),indent=1)
for t,v in out.items():
    if v["coords"] or v["gps"][0]: print(t[:70],"|",v["coords"],v["gps"],v["date"][:20],v["size"])
EOF

Write: elements/snowpeak/src/cam.py
"""cam.py -- perspective camera over a real DEM, with earth curvature and refraction.

At 80 km the earth drops d^2/(2 R_eff) = 440 m below the tangent plane (R_eff = R/(1-k), k = 0.13
standard refraction), so a far peak sits visibly lower and its foothills sink behind the horizon.
We bend the terrain instead of the ray: every sample height is reduced by t_h^2/(2 R_eff), where
t_h is the horizontal distance from the camera.

    C = Camera(e, n, z, az, pitch, hfov, W, H)       # az: compass heading of the view (deg), pitch up +
    G = cast(T, C)                                    # dict of per-pixel buffers (see cast)
"""
import numpy as np
from numba import njit, prange
import numba

R_EFF = 6371000.0 / (1 - 0.13)


class Camera:
    def __init__(self, e, n, z, az, pitch, hfov, W, H, roll=0.0):
        self.e, self.n, self.z = float(e), float(n), float(z)
        self.az, self.pitch, self.hfov, self.W, self.H, self.roll = az, pitch, hfov, W, H, roll

    def rays(self):
        a, p, r = np.radians(self.az), np.radians(self.pitch), np.radians(self.roll)
        fwd = np.array([np.sin(a) * np.cos(p), np.cos(a) * np.cos(p), np.sin(p)])
        right = np.array([np.cos(a), -np.sin(a), 0.0])
        up = np.cross(right, fwd)
        right, up = np.cos(r) * right + np.sin(r) * up, -np.sin(r) * right + np.cos(r) * up
        f = 0.5 * self.W / np.tan(np.radians(self.hfov) / 2)
        xs = (np.arange(self.W) + 0.5 - self.W / 2)
        ys = (self.H / 2 - np.arange(self.H) - 0.5)
        X, Y = np.meshgrid(xs, ys)
        D = fwd[None, None] * f + right[None, None] * X[..., None] + up[None, None] * Y[..., None]
        return D / np.linalg.norm(D, axis=2, keepdims=True)

    def project(self, e, n, z):
        """world -> pixel (x, y); curvature applied"""
        a, p = np.radians(self.az), np.radians(self.pitch)
        fwd = np.array([np.sin(a) * np.cos(p), np.cos(a) * np.cos(p), np.sin(p)])
        right = np.array([np.cos(a), -np.sin(a), 0.0]); up = np.cross(right, fwd)
        dh2 = (e - self.e) ** 2 + (n - self.n) ** 2
        v = np.stack([e - self.e, n - self.n, z - dh2 / (2 * R_EFF) - self.z], -1)
        f = 0.5 * self.W / np.tan(np.radians(self.hfov) / 2)
        zc = v @ fwd
        return self.W / 2 + f * (v @ right) / zc, self.H / 2 - f * (v @ up) / zc

    def to_dict(self):
        return dict(e=self.e, n=self.n, z=self.z, az=self.az, pitch=self.pitch, hfov=self.hfov,
                    W=self.W, H=self.H, roll=self.roll)


@njit(cache=True, fastmath=True)
def _h(Z, x0, y0, res, px, py):
    ny, nx = Z.shape
    c = (px - x0) / res - 0.5; r = (y0 - py) / res - 0.5
    if c < 0 or c >= nx - 1 or r < 0 or r >= ny - 1:
        return -1e9
    ic = int(c); ir = int(r); fc = c - ic; fr = r - ir
    return (Z[ir, ic] * (1 - fc) * (1 - fr) + Z[ir, ic + 1] * fc * (1 - fr)
            + Z[ir + 1, ic] * (1 - fc) * fr + Z[ir + 1, ic + 1] * fc * fr)


@njit(parallel=True, cache=True, fastmath=True)
def _cast(Z, x0, y0, res, ce, cn, cz, D, far, reff, out_t):
    H, W = D.shape[0], D.shape[1]
    for i in prange(H):
        for j in range(W):
            dx, dy, dz = D[i, j, 0], D[i, j, 1], D[i, j, 2]
            hor = np.sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy) + 1e-9
            t = 2.0; tp = 0.0; hit = -1.0
            while t < far:
                px = ce + dx * t; py = cn + dy * t
                th = t * hor
                pz = cz + dz * t + th * th / (2 * reff)      # ray height in the bent-terrain frame
                g = _h(Z, x0, y0, res, px, py)
                if g < -1e8:
                    break
                above = pz - g
                if above <= 0:
                    lo = tp; hi = t                          # bisection refine
                    for _ in range(18):
                        m = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
                        qx = ce + dx * m; qy = cn + dy * m; qh = m * hor
                        qz = cz + dz * m + qh * qh / (2 * reff)
                        if qz - _h(Z, x0, y0, res, qx, qy) <= 0:
                            hi = m
                        else:
                            lo = m
                    hit = hi
                    break
                tp = t
                step = above * 0.5
                smin = 0.35 * res + t * 0.0004
                if step < smin:
                    step = smin
                if step > 0.02 * t + 4 * res:
                    step = 0.02 * t + 4 * res
                t += step
            out_t[i, j] = hit


def cast(T, C, far=250000.0):
    """per-pixel buffers: t (m along ray, -1 = sky), e, n, z (hit, true height), r, c (grid coords)."""
    D = C.rays().astype(np.float64)
    t = np.zeros((C.H, C.W))
    _cast(T["Z"].astype(np.float32), T["x0"], T["y0"], T["res"], C.e, C.n, C.z, D, far, R_EFF, t)
    sky = t < 0
    tt = np.where(sky, 0, t)
    e = C.e + D[..., 0] * tt; n = C.n + D[..., 1] * tt
    r = (T["y0"] - n) / T["res"] - 0.5; c = (e - T["x0"]) / T["res"] - 0.5
    from scipy.ndimage import map_coordinates
    z = map_coordinates(T["Z"], [r.ravel(), c.ravel()], order=1, mode="nearest").reshape(r.shape)
    dist = np.hypot(e - C.e, n - C.n)
    return dict(t=np.where(sky, -1, t), sky=sky, e=e, n=n, z=z, r=r, c=c, dist=np.where(sky, np.inf, dist), D=D)

Bash: Write skyline extractor and test
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/snowpeak/src; cat > skyline.py <<'EOF'
"""photo skyline: for each column, the first row (from the top) where the image leaves the sky.
Sky model: per-row median colour of a sky band; a pixel leaves the sky when its Lab distance from
the sky row colour (blurred) exceeds thr for `run` consecutive rows."""
import numpy as np
from PIL import Image, ImageFilter
from skimage.color import rgb2lab

def photo_skyline(path, W=None, thr=6.0, run=3, top_frac=0.12, blur=1.0, sky_cols=None):
    im = Image.open(path).convert("RGB")
    if W: im = im.resize((W, round(im.size[1] * W / im.size[0])), Image.LANCZOS)
    im = im.filter(ImageFilter.GaussianBlur(blur))
    lab = rgb2lab(np.asarray(im) / 255.0)
    H, Wd = lab.shape[:2]
    # sky colour as a smooth function of row, fitted on the top rows and extrapolated linearly
    k = max(4, int(H * top_frac))
    rows = np.arange(k); cols = sky_cols if sky_cols is not None else slice(None)
    prof = np.median(lab[:k, cols], axis=1)                       # k x 3
    A = np.vstack([rows, np.ones(k)]).T
    coef = np.linalg.lstsq(A, prof, rcond=None)[0]
    skyrow = np.vstack([np.arange(H), np.ones(H)]).T @ coef       # H x 3
    d = np.linalg.norm(lab - skyrow[:, None, :], axis=2)
    out = np.full(Wd, H - 1)
    big = d > thr
    for x in range(Wd):
        col = big[:, x]
        c = np.convolve(col.astype(int), np.ones(run, int), "valid")
        idx = np.nonzero(c >= run)[0]
        if len(idx): out[x] = idx[0]
    return out, d

def render_skyline(sky):
    H, W = sky.shape
    out = np.full(W, H - 1)
    for x in range(W):
        idx = np.nonzero(~sky[:, x])[0]
        if len(idx): out[x] = idx[0]
    return out
EOF

Write: elements/snowpeak/src/fit.py
"""fit.py -- fit a photo's camera (heading, pitch, fov, roll) given its GPS position.

The camera position is fixed by EXIF GPS, so every candidate view is a re-sampling of ONE panorama
rendered from that point: fitting costs image warps, not ray casts.

Score: the photo's edge strength (Lab gradient, distance-smoothed) sampled along the render's
silhouette edges (sky boundary + depth jumps).  Robust to haze, where colour-based sky masks fail.

    P = pano(T, e, n, z, az0, az_span, el_lo, el_hi, dpp)          # dict: dist, az, el grids
    best = fit(photo_path, P, x0=(az, pitch, hfov, roll), W=480)
    view = warp(P, best, W, H)                                       # dist image for that camera
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as ndi
from scipy.optimize import minimize
from PIL import Image, ImageFilter
from skimage.color import rgb2lab
import cam


def pano(T, e, n, z, az0, az_span, el_lo, el_hi, dpp=0.01, far=250000.0):
    W = int(az_span / dpp); H = int((el_hi - el_lo) / dpp)
    az = az0 - az_span / 2 + (np.arange(W) + 0.5) * dpp
    el = el_hi - (np.arange(H) + 0.5) * dpp
    A, E = np.meshgrid(np.radians(az), np.radians(el))
    D = np.stack([np.sin(A) * np.cos(E), np.cos(A) * np.cos(E), np.sin(E)], -1)
    t = np.zeros((H, W))
    cam._cast(T["Z"].astype(np.float32), T["x0"], T["y0"], T["res"], e, n, z, D, far, cam.R_EFF, t)
    sky = t < 0
    tt = np.where(sky, 0, t)
    dist = np.where(sky, np.inf, tt * np.hypot(D[..., 0], D[..., 1]))
    return dict(dist=dist, sky=sky, t=t, az0=az0 - az_span / 2, el0=el_hi, dpp=dpp, e=e, n=n, z=z, D=D)


def view_dirs(p, W, H):
    az, pitch, hfov, roll = p
    C = cam.Camera(0, 0, 0, az, pitch, hfov, W, H, roll)
    D = C.rays()
    a = np.degrees(np.arctan2(D[..., 0], D[..., 1]))
    el = np.degrees(np.arcsin(np.clip(D[..., 2], -1, 1)))
    return a, el


def warp(P, p, W, H, key="dist", order=0):
    a, el = view_dirs(p, W, H)
    col = ((a - P["az0"] + 540) % 360 - 180) / P["dpp"] - 0.5
    row = (P["el0"] - el) / P["dpp"] - 0.5
    src = P[key]
    if key == "dist":
        src = np.where(np.isfinite(src), src, 1e9)
    return ndi.map_coordinates(src, [row, col], order=order, mode="nearest")


def silhouette(dist, jump=1.15):
    """edges: sky boundary and depth discontinuities (farther/nearer ratio > jump)"""
    d = dist
    up = np.roll(d, 1, 0); up[0] = d[0]
    e = (d < 1e8) & ((up >= 1e8) | (up > d * jump))
    return e


def photo_edges(path, W, blur=1.2):
    im = Image.open(path).convert("RGB")
    H = round(im.size[1] * W / im.size[0])
    im = im.resize((W, H), Image.LANCZOS).filter(ImageFilter.GaussianBlur(blur))
    lab = rgb2lab(np.asarray(im) / 255.0)
    g = np.zeros((H, W))
    for k, w in ((0, 1.0), (1, 1.5), (2, 1.5)):
        gy = ndi.sobel(lab[..., k], 0); gx = ndi.sobel(lab[..., k], 1)
        g += w * np.hypot(gx, gy)
    g /= np.percentile(g, 99) + 1e-6
    g = np.clip(g, 0, 1)
    return ndi.grey_dilation(g, size=3), H


def score(p, P, E, W, H, mask=None):
    d = warp(P, p, W, H)
    s = silhouette(d)
    if mask is not None:
        s &= mask
    n = s.sum()
    if n < 20:
        return 1.0
    return -E[s].mean()


def fit(path, P, x0, W=480, mask_fn=None, steps=(3.0, 1.0, 0.3)):
    E, H = photo_edges(path, W)
    Es = [ndi.gaussian_filter(E, s) for s in steps]
    mask = mask_fn(W, H) if mask_fn else None
    x = np.array(x0, float)
    for Ei, s in zip(Es, steps):
        r = minimize(score, x, args=(P, Ei / (Ei.max() + 1e-9), W, H, mask), method="Nelder-Mead",
                     options=dict(xatol=0.005, fatol=1e-4, maxiter=400,
                                  initial_simplex=[x] + [x + np.eye(4)[k] * np.array([0.6, 0.3, 0.6, 0.4])[k] * s / 3
                                                         for k in range(4)]))
        x = r.x
        print(f"  blur {s}: {np.round(x, 3)} score {r.fun:.4f}")
    return x, E, H


def overlay(path, P, p, W, out):
    E, H = photo_edges(path, W)
    im = Image.open(path).convert("RGB").resize((W, H), Image.LANCZOS)
    a = np.asarray(im).copy()
    s = silhouette(warp(P, p, W, H))
    s = ndi.binary_dilation(s)
    a[s] = (255, 40, 40)
    Image.fromarray(a).save(out)

"""sun.py -- solar azimuth/elevation (NOAA simplified algorithm, ~0.5 deg), and sun direction vectors."""
import numpy as np
from datetime import datetime, timezone, timedelta


def position(lat, lon, when_utc):
    """when_utc: datetime (UTC). returns (azimuth deg from N clockwise, elevation deg)"""
    doy = when_utc.timetuple().tm_yday
    hour = when_utc.hour + when_utc.minute / 60 + when_utc.second / 3600
    g = 2 * np.pi / 365 * (doy - 1 + (hour - 12) / 24)
    eqt = 229.18 * (0.000075 + 0.001868 * np.cos(g) - 0.032077 * np.sin(g) - 0.014615 * np.cos(2 * g)
                    - 0.040849 * np.sin(2 * g))
    decl = (0.006918 - 0.399912 * np.cos(g) + 0.070257 * np.sin(g) - 0.006758 * np.cos(2 * g)
            + 0.000907 * np.sin(2 * g) - 0.002697 * np.cos(3 * g) + 0.00148 * np.sin(3 * g))
    tst = hour * 60 + eqt + 4 * lon
    ha = np.radians(tst / 4 - 180)
    la = np.radians(lat)
    cz = np.sin(la) * np.sin(decl) + np.cos(la) * np.cos(decl) * np.cos(ha)
    zen = np.arccos(np.clip(cz, -1, 1))
    az = np.degrees(np.arctan2(np.sin(ha), np.cos(ha) * np.sin(la) - np.tan(decl) * np.cos(la))) + 180
    return az % 360, 90 - np.degrees(zen)


def local(lat, lon, y, mo, d, h, mi=0, utc_offset=-7):
    """local clock time (PDT = -7, PST = -8)"""
    t = datetime(y, mo, d, h, mi, tzinfo=timezone.utc) - timedelta(hours=utc_offset)
    return position(lat, lon, t)


def vec(az, el):
    a, e = np.radians(az), np.radians(el)
    return np.array([np.sin(a) * np.cos(e), np.cos(a) * np.cos(e), np.sin(e)])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(local(47.608, -122.386, 2009, 8, 1, 20, 52))

# Snowpeak notebook

Working thread, newest at the bottom. Images referenced by path under this folder.

## 2026-09-26, day 1

**Reading.** observation TUTORIAL (three-panel sheet, ramp coordinate with a jump, clustered dither),
willow/ground/aerial tutorials, aerial how-to 4 (far view: bands, collapse, cavity tone) and 5
(mountain ground: snow drawn in its shadows; far snow was salt-and-pepper, the weakest cell), the
snow POST (Ferrari: "on mountains the rock defines the snow"; few big shapes; haze must not kill the
subject's rock/snow contrast), rock LESSONS (Dragontail camera fit, IoU 0.94; "value statement taken
from the photo"), naturalist geo 03 and 10.

What the studio already knows that I must not relearn:

- far snow as thresholded noise = salt-and-pepper (aerial). Blur before threshold; far snow needs its own simplification.
- snow is drawn by its shadows; lit snow flat, 1-2 values; shaded snow L* ~76 vs shaded rock ~51 (Sawtooth).
- haze: black point rises, range collapses, b* goes blue (Blue Ridge numbers).
- mountains: build from real profiles, not noise. Here I can do better: real DEMs.

**Data on disk** (~/work/voxsim-data): USGS 1/3" DEM tiles covering all four peaks and Seattle;
ESA WorldCover 2021 (class 70 snow/ice = glacier mask, 10 trees, 60 bare). `src/dem.py` reprojects
any box onto UTM 10N. Quicklook: `img/quicklook_dem_ice.png` (Rainier, Stuart, Olympus, Glacier Peak).

**References.** 20 queries via imgfetch (`refs/`). Commons metadata with camera GPS for the key ones
(`refs/commons_meta.json`). Answer keys chosen:

- Rainier 96 km: "Mount Rainier from Seattle (1)", 2009-08-01 20:52, from Elliott Bay (hazy August evening).
- Rainier 16 km, September minimum: "Rainier20200906", 2020-09-06 19:37, west side, alpenglow.
- Rainier 13 km, late September: "Mount Rainier and Sunrise", from Sunrise (Emmons Glacier, Little Tahoma).
- Rainier 20 km, early July: "from the Silver Queen Peak".
- Stuart 3-6 km: "Mount Stuart 7814p" (July 2008), "sun & fog from near Longs Pass" (Oct 2024).
- Dragontail/Colchuck 2 km, September: "Dragontail Peak, Colchuck Glacier, and Colchuck Peak" (2018-09-03).
- Olympus 26 km: Hurricane Ridge photos (camera 47.9696 -123.4991).
- Olympics 80-100 km: "from Fremont, Seattle" (Jan 2015), "14th Ave E" (Dec 2014).
- Glacier Peak 12-50 km: 7118/7137b (Aug 2003), 8782s (Oct 2013), from Sloan Peak.

**Camera fit.** `src/cam.py` casts rays over the DEM with earth curvature + refraction
(R_eff = R/(1-0.13); at 96 km the terrain drops 630 m). `src/fit.py`: because GPS fixes the camera
position, render one panorama from there and fit heading/pitch/fov/roll by warping it. Score =
photo Lab-edge strength along the render's silhouette edges (colour sky masks failed in the haze:
`stages/t_sk_all.png`, the sky gradient breaks a linear sky model). First fit, Rainier from Elliott
Bay: az 150.63, pitch 1.155, hfov 7.94, roll -0.05. The silhouette lands on the photo's summit,
left shoulder and long right ridge (`stages/fit_sea_rainier.png`). The near shore is too low in the
render: trees (~40 m) are not in a bare-earth DEM.
Bash: Fit camera for the September west-side Rainier photo
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/elements/snowpeak/src; cat > fitcam.py <<'EOF'
"""fitcam.py <name> : fit and store camera for a named answer-key photo (see SHOTS)."""
import numpy as np, dem, fit, sys, json, pickle, os, time
REF="../refs/"
SHOTS={
 "sea_rainier": dict(photo=REF+"Mount_Rainier_from_Seattle/commons_Mount_Rainier_from_Seattle_1_jpg_785aaa4b.jpg",
     cam=(47.607985,-122.386236), agl=None, z=12, peak="rainier", res=30, pad=(15000,25000,20000,5000),
     fov=8.0, span=24, el=(-1.5,4.0), time=(2009,8,1,20,52), maskfrac=0.6),

def box(s):
    ce,cn=dem.ll2utm(*s["cam"]); lat,lon,_=dem.PEAKS[s["peak"]]; pe,pn=dem.ll2utm(lat,lon)
    a,b,c,d=s["pad"]
    return ce,cn,pe,pn,(min(ce,pe)-a,max(ce,pe)+b,min(cn,pn)-c,max(cn,pn)+d)
def setup(name):
    s=SHOTS[name]; ce,cn,pe,pn,bx=box(s)
    T=dem.load_box(*[round(v) for v in bx],s["res"],tag=name)
    z=s.get("z") or float(dem.sample(T,ce,cn)[0])+s["agl"]
    return s,T,ce,cn,pe,pn,z
if __name__=="__main__":
    name=sys.argv[1]; s,T,ce,cn,pe,pn,z=setup(name)
    az=np.degrees(np.arctan2(pe-ce,pn-cn)); d=np.hypot(pe-ce,pn-cn)
    pitch0=np.degrees(np.arctan2(dem.PEAKS[s["peak"]][2]-z-d*d/(2*6371000/0.87),d))*0.6
    fn=f"../cache/pano_{name}.pkl"
    if os.path.exists(fn): P=pickle.load(open(fn,"rb"))
    else:
        t=time.time(); P=fit.pano(T,ce,cn,z,az,s["span"],s["el"][0],s["el"][1],0.02 if s["fov"]>20 else 0.01)
        print("pano",time.time()-t); pickle.dump(P,open(fn,"wb"))
    x0=[float(v) for v in sys.argv[2:6]] if len(sys.argv)>5 else (az,pitch0,s["fov"],0.0)
    mf=s["maskfrac"]
    x,E,H=fit.fit(s["photo"],P,x0,W=480,mask_fn=lambda W,H:(np.arange(H)[:,None]<mf*H)&np.ones((1,W),bool))
    print(name,"dist km %.1f z %.0f"%(d/1000,z),"best",x)
    fit.overlay(s["photo"],P,x,960,f"../stages/fit_{name}.png")
    cams=json.load(open("../cache/cams.json")) if os.path.exists("../cache/cams.json") else {}
    cams[name]=dict(e=ce,n=cn,z=z,az=x[0],pitch=x[1],hfov=x[2],roll=x[3])
    json.dump(cams,open("../cache/cams.json","w"),indent=1)

        R=s["snap"]; r0=int((T["y0"]-cn)/T["res"]); c0=int((ce-T["x0"])/T["res"]); k=int(R/T["res"])

    return s,T,ce,cn,pe,pn,z''')

Bash: Read the new model-systems library

# output:
# The studio library: model systems

The elk and deer student succeeded largely because it didn't start from raw photos. It started from finished analysis:

- Muybridge's frame-by-frame plates;
- the naturalist's "four things that make a 6-pixel elk read".

Natural scientists used to be trained to draw: a geologist could draw a flake, an outcrop or a fold with economy and truth. Naturalist painting guides were built on the same scientific understanding. This library collects those traditions so studies can start where the experts finished, instead of doing a geology PhD from photographs.

**How to use it:**

- Before drawing, find your subject's model system here (or online, below) and read it closely.
- Then build on it, the way the elk student built on Muybridge.
- Open the PDFs and look at the plates; the drawings are the point.

Refetch anything missing with `./fetch.sh` (resumable). Files are also mirrored in S3 under `voxsim/shared/library/`.

## On disk (open access)

| Domain | File | What it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/blockdiagramsoth0000armi_s8g3.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Block Diagrams* (1924).** How geologists draw landforms in 3-D: every landform type as a block diagram, with the drawing method. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/2015.205632.Geomorphology.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Geomorphology* (1939).** Landforms explained through his own drawings: glacial troughs, cirques, rivers, coasts, volcanoes. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/physicalgeograp00davigoog.pdf` | **W. M. Davis, *Physical Geography* (1898).** Davis's block diagrams: how landscapes form and age. |
| Terrain, rock at map scale | `terrain/2015.119840.Cartographic-Relief-Presentation.pdf` | **Imhof, *Cartographic Relief Presentation*.** The rigorous method for showing terrain by light, and Swiss rock drawing: cliffs rendered by structure with a few strokes. Probably the single most useful text for rock at landscape and region scale. |
| Rock, mountains | `rock/modernpainters04rusk.pdf` | **Ruskin, *Modern Painters IV: Of Mountain Beauty* (1856).** A painter's analysis of rock types, cleavage, crests, banks and stones, written for artists and illustrated with his own drawings. |
| Rock, strata at distance | `rock/holmes/*.jpg` | **William Henry Holmes, plates for the Grand Cañon Atlas (1882).** The masterpieces of American scientific landscape drawing: stratified cliffs and plateaus at great distance in a few values and stepped strata lines. Directly relevant to the Yakima basalt. |
| Rock, structure | `rock/reportongeology05duttgoog.pdf` | **G. K. Gilbert, *Geology of the Henry Mountains* (1877).** A classic illustrated geological report. |
| Rock, a flake | `rock/cifa_lithic_illustration_guide.pdf` | **CIfA, *The Illustration of Lithic Artefacts*.** How archaeologists draw a knapped flake: conventions for ridges, ripples and light. The drawing-a-flake skill itself. |
| Trees | `trees/artisticanatomyo1915cole.pdf` | **Rex Vicat Cole, *The Artistic Anatomy

# output:
III. PRECIPICES

237

§ 18. I have thought it worth while, for the sake
of these lessons, and the other interests connected
with them, to lead the reader thus far into the
examination of the principal precipices among the
Alps, although, so far as our immediate purposes are
concerned, the inquiry cannot be very fruitful or
helpful to us. For rocks of this kind, being found
only in the midst of the higher snow fields, are not
only out of the general track of the landscape painter,
but are for the most part quite beyond his power —
even beyond Turner's. The waves of snow, when it
becomes a principal element in mountain form, are at
once so subtle in tone, and so complicated in curve
and fold, that no skill will express them, so as to
keep the whole luminous mass in anything like a
true relation to the rock darkness. For the distant
rocks of the upper peaks are themselves, when in
light, paler than white paper, and their true size
and relation to near objects cannot be exhibited
unless they are painted in the palest tones. Yet, as
compared with their snow, they are so dark that a
daguerreotype taken for the proper number of
seconds to draw the snow shadows rightly, will
always represent the rocks as coal-black. In order,
therefore, to paint a snowy mountain properly, we
should need a light as much brighter than white
paper as white paper is brighter than charcoal. So
that although it is possible, with deep blue sky, and
purple rocks, and blue shadows, to obtain a very
interesting resemblance of snow effect, and a true
one up to a certain point (as in the best examples
of the body-colour drawings sold so extensively in
Switzerland), it is not possible to obtain any of those
refinements of form and gradation which a great
artist's eye

among
highest
hills,requires.

"""possearch.py <shot> [radius_km] : when the GPS tag is unreliable, search camera position.
Candidates: local high points (max within 250 m) on a grid around the GPS fix; each gets a coarse
panorama and the summit-control-point fov scan; best few are refined by fitcam."""
import numpy as np, dem, fit, fitcam, sys, time
name=sys.argv[1]; R=float(sys.argv[2]) if len(sys.argv)>2 else 4
s=fitcam.SHOTS[name]; ce0,cn0,pe,pn,bx=fitcam.box(s)
T=dem.load_box(*[round(v) for v in bx],30,tag=name+"_30")
lat,lon,zs=dem.PEAKS[s["peak"]]; ez,nz=dem.ll2utm(lat,lon)
E,H=fit.photo_edges(s["photo"],320); Eb=fit.ndi.gaussian_filter(E,1.5); Eb/=Eb.max()
M=(np.arange(H)[:,None]<s["maskfrac"]*H)&np.ones((1,320),bool)

for dx in np.arange(-R,R+0.01,1.0):
  for dy in np.arange(-R,R+0.01,1.0):
    e=ce0+dx*1000; n=cn0+dy*1000
    r0=int((T["y0"]-n)/res); c0=int((e-T["x0"])/res); k=8
    w=Z[r0-k:r0+k+1,c0-k:c0+k+1]; i,j=np.unravel_index(np.argmax(w),w.shape)
    e+= (j-k)*res; n-=(i-k)*res; z=float(w[i,j])+3
    dd=np.hypot(ez-e,nz-n); az=np.degrees(np.arctan2(ez-e,nz-n))
    el=np.degrees(np.arctan2(zs-z-dd*dd/(2*6371000/0.87),dd))
    P=fit.pano(T,e,n,z,az,80,el-18,el+8,0.06)
    best=None
    for fv in np.linspace(*s["fovs"],16):
        f=0.5/np.tan(np.radians(fv)/2); ar=H/320
        ax=np.degrees(np.arctan((s["summit"][0]-0.5)/f)); ay=np.degrees(np.arctan((0.5-s["summit"][1])*ar/f))
        p=(az-ax,el-ay,fv,0.0); sc=fit.score(p,P,Eb,320,H,M)
        if best is None or sc<best[0]: best=(sc,p)
    out.append((best[0],e,n,z,best[1])); print(f"{dx:+.0f},{dy:+.0f} z{z:.0f} score {best[0]:.3f} fov {best[1][2]:.1f}",flush=True)
out.sort(key=lambda o:o[0])
for o in out[:5]:
    la,lo=dem.utm2ll(o[1],o[2]); print("TOP",round(o[0],3),round(la,5),round(lo,5),round(o[3]),np.round(o[4],2))
